# SESSION.md

The shared log for four people and their Claude sessions. Read it when you start.
Append to it when you reach a milestone or stop. Edit only your own row in the
table. Everything below "Session entries" is append-only, newest at the bottom.

## To do, in priority order

There is no timeline. The aim is to excel on every judging criterion, whatever
work that takes. Work from the top; when the situation changes, reorder this
list instead of dropping items. The one fixed date is the submission: Sunday
15 Nov, 10:00 Helsinki.

This list assigns nobody. Who does what is decided by the team in person.

**1. The gap (30 points): frontier models must consistently score below the reference.**
- Find a mechanism that makes Fable, Opus and GPT-class models fall short, not only Sonnet. Today no frontier model does.
- Finish and test the three checks being built (`light`, `seated`, `tolerant`).
- Build and test the remaining proposals from the 09:44 entry: a winding stem at right angles (bevel or crown teeth), an intermittent date ring, a change request that edits geometry with "kept" measured by volume.
- Keep looking beyond that list; nothing is ruled out because it is large.
- Test every candidate with clean chains, at least three per model, including GPT in the Harbor sandbox. Keep what bites on the strongest models.

**2. Meaningful difficulty (30 points): every shortfall explained and fair.**
- For each failed check, trace it to a decision the agent made, in the transcript and in the submitted files.
- Confirm each failure against the grader, so a grader fault is never reported as an agent failure.
- Confirm the agent had the information and tools to do better, and that no failure comes from a time limit, trick wording or a broken tool.
- Write the failure analysis, with pictures from `tools/viewer/`.

**3. Reward integrity (25 points): no exploit beats real work.**
- Anchors for every stage and every check, including each new one; all asserted by one script.
- A deliberate exploit hunt against the grader and the STEP loader; every attempt logged with its score, every hit turned into an anchor.
- Do-nothing and stop-early baselines defined, run and in the results table.
- Fix the kept-parts score, which counts files and can be padded.
- Close the open review notes (hard links, project name in file headers, the reference's thin drive margin).

**4. Craft (15 points): it makes sense and anyone can reproduce it.**
- One Harbor task for all stages; reference 1.0 and do-nothing 0.0 in the sandbox; one command in the README. Rebuild the image and rerun both whenever a new check lands.
- A dry run from a clean clone by someone who did not write the README.
- Repeat runs, so every reported number is shown to repeat.
- README in a human voice (the organisers warned that AI-written READMEs read poorly); demo script and video, using `tools/viewer/` to show the reference running and a part that cannot be installed.
- A read of `environment/public/SPEC_WATCH.md` by someone who knows watches, flagging anything a watchmaker would call wrong.

**5. Tiebreaker: a smaller model that improves after training on our environment.**
- This was cut from scope on day one. The organisers name it as the tiebreaker and as something they look for, so it is back on the list; a human should confirm.

- 2026-11-14 14:08 Decided by itswinther, answering the two points in A's 14:05 entry (relayed by the B orchestrator, in itswinther's words as closely as I can keep them). **(a) Scoring rule, for both tasks:** later parts count. The agent is not told whether anything is wrong: whatever it produces is evaluated as a whole and part by part. If one part fails, for example part 3, the run does not get a 1, but a full build still has to be made, because the agent should not know whether it failed. It runs truly sandboxed, with no step-by-step instructions. So there is no in-order gating (a step does not stop counting because an earlier one failed), no verdict or score ever reaches the agent, and the score has a per-part component and a whole-build component. **(b) Crown winding:** it becomes a fifth part of the one job, not a separate task.

## Proposed spec changes
- [ ] (B, 03:2x) DS-W04 tooth-load factor for friction, 1.45 dry (from A's train sim, a/train-sim 42b3eaf: dry-friction peak tooth force 1.39-1.45 x the Lewis F). Strength check uses F x factor. Reference margin 1.92 still passes.

Append here. A human has to say yes before SPEC.md changes.

- 2026-11-14 01:55 **Clarifications SPEC 0.1 leaves open.** Proposed by itswinther + Claude (B). Status: awaiting yes/no. The grader already uses these values; each is one line in `geometry/constants.py` marked INTERPRETATION, so a no is cheap.
  - `assembly.json` format: `{"board": [...], "lid": [...], "battery": [...], "door": [...]}`, each a list of 4x4 row-major matrices mapping the body from its supplied frame to the world. Board, lid and door are supplied in their final pose, so their last pose is the identity. The battery's own frame is x 0..48, y 0..30, z 0..7.5; its lead face is x = 0 and its top face z = 7.5.
  - Keep-outs are centred on the connector body's section and start at its outer face: J1 y 16.25..28.75, z -0.25..6.75, x -1.5..-21.5; J2 centre (y 12, z -2.5), x 72.5..92.5. "Grown by r" is a rectangle offset by r (square corners) for J1 and a larger circle for J2.
  - "Wall mid-plane" for K3/K4 is a fixed plane: x = -1.05 for J1, x = 72.55 for J2.
  - G5: the "1.0 sphere" is a diameter. "The cavity" is anywhere within 1.5 of board A or the battery. The two windows are plugged with the grown-by-3 sections for x <= 0 (J1) and x >= 70 (J2).
  - Contract rule 1: "at least 30 from its final pose" is measured at the body's vertex centroid. "Wholly outside the envelope" means zero intersection volume with the envelope box.
  - F1 is measured on a slice at z = -0.05, over the ring between dia 3.2 and dia 7. F4 is the exact minimum surface distance. F5 needs a 48 x 30 face horizontal in the final pose.
  - W1/W2 voxel margins: a wall of 1.6 always passes, the allowed deviation is 0.5 + one 0.2 voxel = 0.7. In exact maths a sharp 90-degree solid corner sits 0.586 from the opened shape, so the bare 0.5 in the spec would fail every sharp box corner.
  - Reference volume (base + door) is 15072.56 mm3, so the material budget is 17333.44 mm3.
- 2026-11-14 09:44 **Make the staged watch harder through CAD difficulty.** Proposed by itswinther + Claude (B), after a search of 2026 CAD-agent benchmarks (sources in the entry of the same time). Status: awaiting a human yes/no per item; nothing is built. Ranked by expected effect for the build cost.
  1. **Mass budget that forces skeletonised wheels and plates.** State a maximum volume per wheel (or for the whole movement) that solid discs cannot meet, plus a minimum radius for concave corners. Wheels need spokes and rims near the 0.2 thickness limit. Why: in last night's chains all three models said in their reports that they had not run the thickness test (and Sonnet not the collision test) and passed only because their parts were chunky. Cost: volume is one line in the grader; the corner rule reuses the voxel code; the reference needs spoked wheels.
  2. **Assemble every part for real.** Drop the rule that parts of one mobile are not tested against each other at stage 4, and test "held" per part, not per mobile. Every wheel then needs a shoulder to sit on and something that retains it, designed at stage 1 and only tested at stage 4. Why: the only shortfall on two nights is a design that passes its own stage and cannot be assembled later. Cost: small in the grader, moderate in the reference.
  3. **A winding stem at right angles (new stage).** A stem enters through the case side and must turn the barrel through a crown or bevel pair, inside the frozen movement. Teeth on non-parallel axes cannot be made by extruding a 2D profile; they need lofted or generated surfaces that survive the STEP validity check. Why: published benchmarks report the largest drops on loft and sweep features and on gear mechanisms. Cost: high (mesh test for arbitrary axes, a bevel maker for the reference); probe with one single-stage clean run before building the stage.
  4. **The train must run with the arbors slightly out of place.** Repeat the mesh test with each driven arbor moved 0.03 towards and away from its driver. Why: it asks for real backlash and tooth depth, not nominal geometry; no published CAD benchmark tests tolerances. Cost: two more mesh runs per pair.
  5. **An intermittent date ring (new stage).** A ring that advances one step per day and is locked in between (Geneva-type, rigid parts only), graded by finding the collision-free angle of the driven part at each step. Cost: high (a contact-driven replay is new engine code).
  6. **Change request that edits geometry, with "kept" measured by volume.** Replace byte-identical kept files by the share of received material still in place, and choose a change that moves an axis (small seconds at 6 o'clock), so plates must be re-bored, not swapped. Fixes the open point that the kept share counts files.
  Not proposed: drawings as input (the one benchmark that splits it shows image-to-CAD as its easiest track, and we do not know that the judged agent sees images); a physics or FEA grader (the agent cannot rebuild it exactly, so differences look like noise); judged rubrics; hidden requirements.
- 2026-11-14 11:50 **Close the grader exploits found by the review.** Proposed by itswinther + Claude (review session). Status: **all seven approved by itswinther, 14 Nov 11:58**, to be built by the "Grader exploits fixes" session on top of B's `light`/`seated`/`tolerant` (branch `claude/confident-brown-ipeiud`). Each goes under Decisions with old and new rule when it lands in `SPEC_WATCH.md`; numbers for 5 and 6 are measured first. Full reasoning, reproduction and recommended rules in `HANDOFF_EXPLOITS.md`.
  1. Mesh test: two more no-jam poses after every eighth step, 1/3 and 1/5 of a grid step further on, so the test cannot be passed by tooth counts that differ from the real ones.
  2. Assembled (hard): the fixed parts form one frame (joined when their smallest distance is at most 0.01; the graph of joined parts is connected). The reference's barrel cock needs a foot.
  3. The train runs: every mobile is driven from the barrel through passing meshes. Held: only the frame holds, directly or through mobiles on the same axis that are themselves held in that direction; mobiles on another axis never count.
  4. Stage 3 kept share by volume, not by file, separately for fixed and for mobile parts, the smaller of the two against the reference's.

- 2026-11-14 12:40 **Grade the watch as a whole: an owner's acceptance test, scored in order.** Proposed by Mikael + Claude (A). Status: awaiting yes/no from the team; being tried first in `spikes/keyless/`, nothing in B's task changed.
  - Why: today a design earns most of its points part by part. A gear train with a winding mechanism that cannot hold its wind (no click, so the mainspring unwinds back through the crown) still scores high. GPT trial 1 in `spikes/right_angle/` did exactly that and scored 1.0, because that spike never asked for a click (see its README, "Known spec weakness"). A watch that stops when you let go of the crown is not most of a watch.
  - Proposed spec wording: "The movement is graded the way a watchmaker accepts a finished watch: by using it. The grader runs one fixed sequence of operations on the assembled movement, in order, the way an owner would. A step only counts if every step before it passed. Part-level checks (clearances, support, printability) are conditions on the whole; they do not earn points on their own."
  - Sequence, from a flat mainspring: (1) wind: crown in, turned forward N turns, the mainspring stores the stated turns; (2) hold: let go, the click holds, nothing spins back; (3) run: the train drives the hands at the right rates for at least the stated power reserve, from the stored turns; (4) backward: crown turned backward, nothing winds or jams, stored turns stay; (5) set: crown pulled and turned, the hands move, the barrel does not unwind; (6) return: pushed in and wound again, never half-engaged on the way.
  - Score: consecutive steps passed from step 1 divided by the number of steps, times the part-level conditions. No click gives about 0.17, with a one-line reason anyone can check ("it cannot hold its wind").
  - What it serves: meaningful difficulty (every shortfall has an owner's-eye explanation), the gap (it rewards how parts work together over time, which is where every frontier shortfall so far came from: decisions that pass locally and fail later), and reward integrity (part-level tricks earn nothing unless the whole works).
  - What it needs in B's task: a winding stage (the keyless works: crown, stem, sliding pinion with Breguet teeth, crown and ratchet wheels, click, setting lever; prototype in `spikes/keyless/`), a stated mainspring model (capacity in turns, running at the barrel's stated speed) and the in-order scoring. The existing stage 4 assembly test fits as step 0: it has to assemble before it can be used.
  - Real-world basis: the end-of-line functional test of a finished watch (wind, check it holds, check it runs, set the hands). Results of the keyless trial with old and new scoring will be posted here.
- 2026-11-14 13:26 **One realistic start-to-finish task instead of four frozen prompts.** Direction from itswinther after a conversation with a contact in the industry; written up by the B orchestrator. Status: direction given by itswinther; the three choices marked "needs a yes" below are open; nothing is built. It overlaps with A's `TASK_DOCS_TEMPLATE.md`, `TEST_ENVIRONMENT.md` and `spikes/STAGED_ACCEPTANCE_PLAN.md`, which already carry most of it.
  - What itswinther asked for: no forcing of failures, a realistic task; Harbor's own task format; one brief from start to finish as a professional would get it, not a prompt per stage; keep the four stages but do not freeze them; remove the hints, give context only ("you are a watchmaking professional"); 8 runs per model, in Docker and Modal sandboxes; per task one context file, one prompt file, one solution file with the range each answer must fall in, one step-by-step solution file; weighted steps. The contact's observation: models fail at parsing data and hallucinate when they have to read a full specification to build a system or 3D mechanism.
  - Where A already matches: the four documents (`CONTEXT.md`, `PROMPT.md`, `SOLUTION.md`, `STEP_BY_STEP.md`), Harbor layout and validation ladder, 8 runs per model, a brief that says what to deliver and never how.
  - Where A's plan differs from this direction: it keeps frozen stages (or a change request after delivery, which is a second prompt) and adds a keyless stage and an in-order owner's acceptance test.
  - Proposed integration for the watch task: (1) one Harbor task, one `instruction.md` generated from `PROMPT.md`; (2) the four stages become four weighted parts of one acceptance run on one delivered movement (going train and hands; 24-hour hand; the barrel variant; assembly order and paths), graded by the existing engine in one verifier call; (3) every method hint leaves the agent's text (for example "Read the later stages too", "So a wheel needs a bore that fits its arbor and a shoulder to rest against", "a fit needs a small radial clearance", "leave a little margin", "leaves no room for what the later stages need", "submit a first version early", the reference's kept share); every graded number stays; (4) limits come from real movements or real process data, not from our reference plus a margin: `light` at reference plus 10 percent is the clearest case of a rule sized to bite and is replaced or dropped; (5) the agent time limit is set so that it never decides a score.
  - Needs a yes, 1: what the change stage becomes without freezing. (a) A variant in the same brief: deliver the movement for the 8-hour barrel and a variant for the 6-hour barrel, scored on the variant working and on the parts the two share. (b) A's version: a change request that arrives after delivery, as a second step. (c) Drop it. Recommended: (a).
  - Needs a yes, 2: supplying the requirements as the documents a professional receives (barrel and mainspring datasheet, case drawing or table, process sheet for the printer, a file format sheet) and a short brief on top. This is what would test the contact's observation, and it reverses the day-one decision that cut the datasheet lookup step.
  - Needs a yes, 3: the weights of the four parts and whether a later part counts when an earlier one failed.
  - Consequences to accept knowingly: (i) the frozen hand-over is the only mechanism that has produced a shortfall on a frontier model (GPT-6.1-sol 0.42 in one of two chains; Sonnet 0.47), and A's unfrozen five-step spike gave GPT 1.0 three times, so scores of 1.0 are the likely outcome unless reading a full realistic specification is what trips the models; (ii) the stage-2 and stage-4 "locked" gates and the stage-3 kept share have no meaning when nothing is frozen, which touches fix 4 of the exploits session.

- 2026-11-15 02:20 **Thinness probe, then a thin, requirements-only task: a gap lever.** Proposed by itswinther + Claude (brainstorm session; itswinther chose every item below in that session). Status: **probe approved by itswinther, to run now; the final-task items await a team yes.** Probe brief draft: `spikes/thin_probe/BRIEF_DRAFT.md`.
  - Why: frontier models score 1.0 on Parts 1 to 4 (10 of 10). Two industry habits make a gap that is real and explainable: grade the requirement, not a published test (today models rebuild QA-W100's exact sampling as their own self-test), and score a continuous metric against an expert baseline (models meet constraints but rarely optimise hard).
  - **Probe (now):** one run each of Fable 5.2, Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1-sol, locally (`run_clean_job.sh`, Codex for GPT), on a probe branch. QA-W100 is rewritten as requirements only, every number kept and restated as a requirement, with no test methods; this is a quick draft, not reviewed. The order asks for a slim calibre; QA-W100 states a 9.0 mm target and the formula. Grader, barrel BR-096-20, reference and the 3 h limit are unchanged; the thickness score is computed afterwards from the details file. Collect: height and every check result; time used against 3 h; where and why the agent stopped thinning; its own claimed height (`report.json` `height_mm`, ignored by the grader). Promising if frontier runs break requirements while thinning, or stop well above the target.
  - **Thickness score:** H = the total height along z, barrel included, of the taller build (and the keyless works once Part 5 is on). x = (H - target) / (case inner height - target), clamped to 0..1; s = (1 - x)^4. itswinther asked for harsher than (1 - x)^2: a 10 mm result should not score well. With a 9.0 mm target, a design that passes everything scores 1.0 at 9.0 mm, 0.81 at 9.5, 0.68 at 10, 0.54 at 11, and 0.50 at the old reference's 13.1. The agent sees the formula, not a table.
  - **Reward:** 1/2 x (share of requirements met) + 1/2 x [every requirement met] x s, times the statement factor. Thinness only counts for a watch that works.
  - **Final task, after the probe:** (1) QA-W100 requirements only, numbers restated as requirements (the 0.01 mm3 allowance as "collide", backlash as a quarter pitch, and so on). (2) A minimum wall for load-bearing plates and bridges, so thin frames stay credible. (3) The agent chooses its barrel from a retuned DS-W01: two or three barrels qualify for classes B and D, and a slimmer one costs a smaller module (gear strength binds) and an awkward tooth count (the train must be re-solved). Each barrel needs a mesh and an "as supplied" table, and the variant uses the same barrel. (4) A new expert reference in a real ultra-thin layout, aiming at 9 to 10 mm. The stated target is what it reaches; it must reach it, or the target reads as a trick. (5) The print tolerance waits until after thinness.
  - **Gap bar:** Fable, Opus and GPT each average under 0.85 over 3 runs; the reference scores 1.0; every shortfall is traced to a design decision. Runs that hit the 3 h limit are not counted as gap evidence. Report the raw heights next to the scores, so the gap is not just the exponent.
  - Also decided in the interview: (a) the two levers, requirements-only QA-W100 and thinness, change together, in one rebuild and one pilot; attribution comes from the per-check details and the height. (b) No side probes tonight, thinness only: no crown-winding runs for Fable or Opus, no regrade of old runs alongside. (c) The ten 1.0 deliveries of the one-job task probably still exist on someone's machine (itswinther is not sure); worth finding for a later regrade with checks 1, 6 and 14 fixed. (d) Every frontier model can be run; itswinther says when.
  - Not decided: the minimum-wall value, the retuned barrel rows, and who builds the thin reference.

## Ideas for later

Parked ideas. Not proposals: nothing here changes SPEC.md. Move one to "Proposed spec changes" to put it to the team.
- 2026-11-14 16:15 (A, Mikael) **Parked (on ice): hacking seconds as an upgrade to the crown-winding part, used only if the first runs show crown winding is too easy.** Pulling the crown to the time-setting position swings a stop (hack) lever against the balance or seconds train, so the seconds stop and the time can be set to the second; pushing it in releases it. Real basis: MIL-W-46374F §3.2.3.3.1 ("pulling the stem to setting stops the movement, pushing it in restarts the watch", public domain); Seiko 6R15 technical guide (stop lever 0601 183, time setting with the second hand stopped). Why it fits: the same pull must move the clutch to setting and bring a second lever into the train, a position-dependent contact behaviour whose failures (lever does not reach, rubs while running, jams the pull) follow from geometry rather than from a stated rule. Grading, with A's contact engine: "AT-7 hack": in position 2 the running train is blocked by contact (seconds still); in position 1 it runs with no drag; never jammed between. Cost: the engine needs a train stand-in driven by the mainspring during the check (today the going train is one fixed body in the keyless steps); it fits best in the joined five-part job, where B's train exists. Related candidates in the same family: date quick-set (a middle crown position, a jumper spring; the 6R15 has one), and B's escapement interface.
- 2026-11-14 17:10 (A, Mikael) **Parked (on ice), with hacking: draw the keyless springs as real parts.** Today the click spring, the sliding-pinion (yoke) spring and the setting-lever spring are not drawn: the agent declares a mobile as kind `spring` and the rig applies a light push. In a real watch they are thin bent steel strips that must fit, be anchored to a post, and touch the part they push without colliding with anything. Upgrade: require each spring as a drawn part, its shape at rest, and check fit (no collision at rest and through the acceptance motion, anchored to a fixed part, in contact with its driven part); the push itself stays simulated as now, since the engine does not flex parts. The mainspring stays a supplied, bought-in part (selected from DS-101, as in industry practice), not drawn. Use only if the first runs show crown winding is too easy. Cost: fit checks only; no new physics.

- 2026-11-14 01:25 **"No screws allowed": every body clicks into place.** Proposed by Yoseph + Claude. Status: parked as an idea for later, not proposed to the team. Would need its own spec version if ever adopted.
  - **Why:** SPEC 0.1 says "screws are out of scope, parts are assumed screwed down", so nothing holds the board, lid or door. Tool-free snap-fit assembly is a real design-for-assembly rule, gives a better story for judges, and adds a constraint that interacts with the ones we already have (see "Interactions").
  - **Replaces** the Limits line "Screws are out of scope ... no check tests retention against a pull" with: "No fasteners, glue or press-fit pins. Board A, lid and door must each be held by printed geometry. The battery is held by the door (F3, unchanged)."
  - **How a snap is modelled while staying rigid-body (the hard part).** A snap arm has to bend while it is inserted, but the replay only moves rigid bodies. So the agent declares its snap arms in `assembly.json`, and gives a bend angle per pose:
    `"arms": [{"name": "lid_px", "part": "base", "box": [[x0,y0,z0],[x1,y1,z1]], "root": "-z", "bend": "+x"}]`, and each pose may carry `"arms": {"lid_px": 0.04}` (radians).
    The grader takes the printed material inside `box` as a separate rigid piece. At each pose it rotates that piece about the box's root edge by the declared angle, then applies the normal 0.2 mm3 intersection rule against everything else, the rest of its own part included. This is the pseudo-rigid-body model, simplified to a pivot at the root, which is conservative.
  - **What the grader checks on each arm (the agent's declaration is never trusted):**
    1. Material inside the box touches the rest of the part only across the root face. Voxel test, 0.2 voxels, so a gap needs to be at least 0.4 wide to count.
    2. Strain: the grader measures t at the root and L along the box itself. With tip deflection y = L·sin(angle), max strain is 3·t·y / (2·L²), and it must stay at or below 2.0 % (PLA, assumed). For example, t 1.6 with L 10 allows y of 0.83 mm; L 8 allows only 0.53.
  - **Contract additions (format errors fail G4):** at most 8 arms; arm boxes may not overlap; arms only on printed parts; every angle is 0 in the final pose of every body; a change in angle moves the arm tip at most 0.5 per step (same as rule 3).
  - **Where a broken arm counts:** over-strain or a non-free arm fails the path check (A1 to A4) of the body that was moving at that pose, not a gate. This keeps the decision "paths are weighted checks, not gates".
  - **New checks** (each weight 1; the spec goes from 19 checks / weight 23 to 22 checks / weight 26):
    - R1 Board retained: moving board A 0.6 along +Z makes its bare PCB intersect printed parts by more than 0.2 mm3.
    - R2 Lid retained: moving the lid 0.5 along +Z intersects the base by more than 0.2 mm3.
    - R3 Door retained: moving the door 0.5 along -Z intersects the base by more than 0.2 mm3.
  - **Envelope:** the lid is a supplied flat plate whose top is at z 15.5, so a hook over it needs z max to go from 12.5 to 17.25 (15.5 + 1.6 lip + 0.15).
  - **Interactions that make it harder:** the gap around an arm has to be at least 0.4 to count as free (check 1) but below 1.0 or shielded, or G5's 1.0 sphere gets through; arms must be at least 1.6 thick (W1) yet long enough to flex; board hooks must stay 0.4 from J1, J2 and C1 (F4) and must not catch during the tilt-and-slide path; the door latch must clear the battery (A3, F5).
  - **Anchors to add:** pegs but no hooks (fails R1 to R3); an arm declared over a solid wall (fails A-path by check 1); a stubby arm bent too far (fails A-path by strain); hooks that never deflect (fails A-path by intersection).
  - **Tools:** `sweep` also returns the peak strain per arm and its pose index (a geometry fact, no pass/fail). `get_spec` publishes the strain limit and the arm format.
  - **Cost:** B adds arm extraction, rotation and strain to the shared geometry module, plus three latch sets in the reference, roughly half a day. A adds an `arms` passthrough in `sweep`. D adds four anchors.
  - **Timing:** nothing is built yet, and arms change the core of the path replay. So the cheapest moment to decide is before B writes the replay code, which is tonight. If the team would rather not risk the Sat 07:00 spike, the fallback is to keep it as the difficulty lever for Sat 09:00, used if H1 turns out too easy for the judged agent.
  - **Not verified:** that a reference with latches can pass every gate and check; the 2.0 % strain limit for PLA; that 0.2 voxels resolve the arm gap reliably.
  - **Review by B (itswinther + Claude), 01:35, by hand only:** as written it likely makes H1 easier, because a 20 mm tall wall section declared as an arm may flex 3.3 mm and the board needs only 1.7 mm to drop straight in; the lid hook has no room in x/y (the lid covers the whole footprint); rotating a rigid arm about its root edge intrudes about 0.3 mm3 into its own part. A rigid-only version (door on rails, R3 only) needs no arm code.
- 2026-11-14 03:30 **Mechanical watch gear train: a new challenge, as an alternative to Moving Day.** Proposed by Abhishek + Claude. Status: parked as an idea, nothing built or run. Full write-up in `MECHANICAL_WATCH_IDEA.md`. In short: the agent gets a minimal brief ("design the gear train of a mechanical watch, driven by this barrel turning once per 8 h") and must infer the unstated but universal rules (hour 1 per 12 h, minute 1 per h, seconds 1 per min, clockwise, coaxial hands). The grader checks exact ratios from declared tooth counts, then rotates the train with the existing rigid-pose replay and overlap test (no jam, still engaged, teeth drive, hands clear). Estimated 1.5 to 2 person-days; the reference movement is the critical path.

One rigid arbor with a wide collar below and a wide head above a closed bore cannot be separated from the plate, so whichever of `arb_a` and `day_cock` comes second cannot be installed. Stage 2 passed because "held" wants exactly such a collar and head and assembly is not tested there; the stage-2 instruction and spec did say the movement would be assembled part by part. ### 2026-11-14 09:44 | B: research on CAD difficulty, six proposals | itswinther + Claude
- Did: itswinther asked to keep the staged watch as the base and make it harder through CAD difficulty. Read A's `spikes/RESEARCH_GAPS.md`, searched for 2026 benchmarks of CAD-writing agents, and wrote six ranked proposals under "Proposed spec changes". Nothing was built or run.
- What the search found (from search summaries only: `arxiv.org` and `icml.cc` are not reachable from this session, so no paper was read in full and the numbers are unchecked):
  - CAD-bench (ICML 2026 workshop, cad-bench.github.io): 17 execution-based tasks including threaded mating pairs and gear assemblies, checked with rigid-body simulation; agents "still fail frequently on interfaces, standards-like details and mechanisms".
  - Text2CAD-Bench (arXiv 2605.18430): four tiers; performance "degrades substantially" on the tier with sweep, loft and shell.
  - CADEngBench (arXiv 2608.09296): of 2,400 generated programs 1,030 run and 432 of those pass the next engineering level; edits coupled to existing structure, exact face and edge grounding in assemblies and matched FEA stay hard.
  - RealCADBench (arXiv 2609.03773): agents executed all 25 assembly tasks with surface overlap of about 0.10 to 0.12; execution gets repaired, fine structure and placement do not.
  - Parametric CAD Bench v3 (arXiv 2611.02659): 100 FreeCAD tasks, agents with tools; best system 61%; text creation and create-and-edit in the low 40s for Opus 5.5 while image-to-CAD is above 80.
  - "Done Is Not Correct" (arXiv 2611.10063): single-shot agents claimed completion on 98% of attempts and were silently wrong on 14.1%; a loop that forces measuring before claiming cut that to 3.2%.
- Reading, together with A's notes and our runs: gaps close when the agent can measure exactly and cheaply. What stays open is geometry that is hard to construct in a kernel (non-extruded surfaces), checks that are expensive to run oneself, and consequences that appear stages later.
- Not verified, or assumed: every number above is from a search summary. No proposal has been tried with a clean agent; by last night's rule each needs a clean run before it is trusted.
- Files changed: SESSION.md (Proposed spec changes, this entry).
- Need from others: a human yes/no per proposal; allow `arxiv.org` in the session's network settings if the papers should be read in full.

- Did: timed every gate and check of the grader on the reference's stage 1, on an idle 4-core machine with 15 GB, by wrapping `_run` in `tests/grade_watch.py` from a throwaway script. No file in the repo was changed.
- Verified by (command and result): `python3 -I` script calling `grade_watch.grade(OUT/stage1, 1, None)`: reward 1.0, total 274.6 s. Split: `printable` 156.8 s (57 percent), the mesh runs over all gear pairs 91.1 s (33 percent), reading and tessellating the STEP files 20.1 s (7 percent), `clear` 4.1 s, `spin` 1.1 s, `held` 1.1 s, everything else under 0.1 s.
- Finding: 90 percent of the time is two loops that run one item after another on one core: the thickness test per part (the three large plates dominate) and the mesh test per gear pair. Both are independent per item, so worker processes should cut a design stage from about 4.5 minutes to roughly 1.5 on four cores without changing any number. This matters more once `tolerant` lands, because it runs the mesh test three times per pair (about 270 s serial on this reference).
- For whoever packages the task in Harbor: a full chain is about 14 minutes of verifier time today (233 + 289 + 305 + 28 s). The judges rerun a reference, three agent runs, do-nothing and stop-early; do-nothing grades in under a second.
- Not verified, or assumed: the speed-up estimate is arithmetic, not a measurement. I did not change the grader, because the orchestrator's builders are editing `tests/grade_watch.py` and `geometry/watch.py` now (builder TOLERANT is already asked for a faster `mesh_run`).

Ran the oracle on stage 1 and one GPT chain.
- Why one task per stage: in Harbor a separate verifier sees only its own step's artifacts, and on every step but the last the agent container keeps running beside it (the grader needs about 5.7 GB; Docker here has 7.7 GB). Stage N+1 is built on the host from Harbor's collected `artifacts/app/submission` of stage N; that copy goes into the agent image (`work/`, `previous/`, via B's `make_bundle_watch.py`) and into `tests/prev`, which only the verifier sees and which the grader gets as `--prev`.
- Sandbox: agent allowlist `chatgpt.com` only, Codex pre-installed, `--ak web_search=disabled` (confirmed as `-c web_search=disabled` in the launched `codex exec` command); verifier separate, `no-network`, 7 GB, 15 min. - Did: built `spikes/right_angle/` (B's proposal 3 as a separate spike; nothing in B's task changed). The frozen movement is B's reference stage 1, exported to STL with the main plate bored at the barrel axis, plus a supplied square on the barrel arbor under the plate. The agent designs the winding works on the underside: a stem along a stated x line through the case wall, a right-angle mesh (face/contrate or bevel), optional intermediate wheels, a ratchet on the square, and bridges and posts. The brief states every rule and number.
- Grader: a general-axis kinematic engine on manifold3d. It runs with `python -I`, opens only named files, writes reward 0 first and puts each check in its own try. Each mesh is sampled through a full turn of its slower wheel at 6 samples per driver tooth. The overlap must stay at or below 0.002 mm3, and turning the driven wheel back by 3 % of its pitch must give at least 0.0002 mm3 of contact. Hard requirements: files, and no overlap with the frozen movement at rest. Weighted checks (12): drive 3, direction 1, ratio 1, clear 2, held 2, seated 1, anchored 1, bounds 1.
- Reference: a 12-tooth stem pinion drives a 36-tooth face gear cut by envelope generation, and its 12-tooth pinion drives a 36-tooth ratchet, giving 9 stem turns per arbor turn. It scores 1.0 and grades in about 4 s.

### 2026-11-14 15:00 | A: mentor feedback on the watch idea (from Mikael's recording), and what it means for us | Mikael + Claude
Source: a 7-minute recording of Mikael's team asking a hackathon mentor for feedback, transcribed locally (Whisper, in Docker; the audio and full transcript stay off the repo). Summary of what the mentor said:
- **Hints and fidelity:** "a very fine line between unfairness and hiding too much from the agent, and being too verbose. Think about how it would work in real life." Implied requirements (e.g. "it must keep time") are fine if a real engineer would assume them. This matches our engineer brief plus datasheets plus stated acceptance test.
- **Per-part grading and reward hacking:** "If you grade four different parts, the model might learn to skip one of the hardest parts and optimise the other three, and get 0.75 every time." Also: pointing out reward-hacking behaviour you observe counts in your favour.
- **Harbor:** follow their cookbook; "use Docker Compose within Harbor", the easiest way to build an overall environment.
- **Logs and screenshots of runs** ("pinpoint what went wrong"): "a great idea"; "you guys are on a good track".
- The mentor is reachable on the Discord and in person tonight and early tomorrow morning.

What it means:
1. **The 0.75 concern is answered by the whole-build term, and we should say so in SOLUTION.md and the README.** With B's 14:08 scoring (0.5 × weighted parts + 0.5 × whole build), skipping the hardest part (crown winding, weight 0.25) gives 0.5 × 0.75 + 0 = 0.375, not 0.75, while finishing everything gives 1.0. The gap between "skip the hardest part" and "do it all" is 0.625, so the best strategy is still to do the whole job. Keep the whole-build term at 0.5 or more. Pure per-part averaging (no whole term) would have the problem the mentor describes. Add an anchor "every part except the hardest" to both halves and report its score.
2. **Report observed reward hacking explicitly:** keep the reward-hacks count in the results, add the deliberate cheat run, and write up anything seen.
3. **Docker Compose:** our tasks use a Dockerfile plus Harbor's separate verifier, which is the cookbook's single-container path. Compose matters if we add services (e.g. a sidecar). Check the cookbook's compose examples and follow them if we need more than one service. Not urgent.
4. **Use the mentor** for the four organiser questions in TEST_ENVIRONMENT.md (judged model, timeout and rerun budget, Harbor version, credits) and for a quick look at failure analyses.

  - **Status against the direction.** The direction is agreed on paper (Decisions 13:36, 14:08, 15:44). On `origin/main` at 3bae88f, what a judge opens is still the frozen version: `README.md` describes four frozen stages, `SPEC_WATCH.md` and `INSTRUCTION_WATCH_S1..S4.md` say "stage N of 4", `harbor/watch4/` is one Harbor task per stage. The one-job grader and `PROMPT.md` are in builder worktrees G and R, not on main. No `CONTEXT.md`, `PROMPT.md`, `SOLUTION.md` or `STEP_BY_STEP.md` exists for the watch. A's realistic sheets (several variants, 1:1 and scaled columns, footnotes) enter only with crown winding, whose geometry is not agreed. A realistic way to test reading, without forcing anything: a short work order naming part numbers, plus a supplier-style package (barrel catalogue with several variants, case sheet with several case references, printer process sheet, escapement module sheet), with every graded value traced to one cell as `TRACEABILITY.md` already does. The cheapest evidence on whether it creates a gap: the same grader with the requirements as a spec list versus as the datasheet package, 8 runs each on one frontier model.
  - **Realism points a watchmaker would raise.** (a) No escapement: a real train job ends at the escape pinion at a stated beat rate (vph), and that unit conversion is a classic misread. (b) `CASE_HEIGHT` 13.4 is the reference plus 0.3; a 2824-2 at s = 1.617 is 4.60 x 1.617 = 7.4 mm (A's `TRACEABILITY.md` line 34 notes the same). `CASE_RADIUS` 20.7 does match the 41.4 mm demonstration-scale calibre. (c) The 6-hour variant reads most naturally as "the calibre must accept either of the supplier's two catalogue barrels". (d) Unstated hand speeds are professional knowledge and fit the direction.

The 13:26 entry lists them for removal; a banned-phrase check in the bundle builder and a read of `PROMPT.md` by someone outside the team would show they stay out.
  - **Harbor layout.** A judge would expect one folder, e.g. `tasks/<name>/` with `instruction.md`, `task.toml` (TB2 metadata keys: difficulty, category, expert and junior time estimates; separate verifier), `environment/Dockerfile`, `solution/solve.sh` with `SOLUTION.md` and `STEP_BY_STEP.md`, `tests/test.sh`, and `CONTEXT.md` as the task README. Harbor's RewardKit `reward.toml` (`HARBOR_RESEARCH.md` 1.1) is a Harbor-native place where the step weights would be visible. The retired tasks (H1, H2, Moving Day, Hold the Line), `harbor/watch4/` and the frozen README would read more clearly in an archive folder.

- Honest state of the keyless reference (v1): it follows a real keyless architecture (sliding pinion with Breguet teeth on a square stem, winding pinion, crown wheel through 90°, ratchet and click, setting wheel engaged by the pull, return spring). Its numbers were chosen to pass the v1 rig: 8 Breguet teeth, a 12:36 face gear, a 24-tooth ratchet, 0.012 clearance. It does not use the datasheets, which come with v2. Grossmann's real keyless train has different proportions (sliding pinion 16, crown wheel 42, ratchet 44, winding pinion 18).

  - choose the mainspring from DS-101 for the barrel space and at least 38 h;
  - size the ratchet, click, Breguet teeth and wheels so that stress under DS-101's torque (at s, including the bridle slipping torque) stays within DS-201's allowables;
  - keep the crown torque within DS-401 and DS-501, and fit the pivots to DS-301;
  - record every choice, with the datasheet cell it comes from, in STEP_BY_STEP.md;

### 2026-11-14 17:49 | B: fifth part started; options to make the job harder, for itswinther | itswinther + Claude
- Did: itswinther asked (17:44) to add crown winding now and to think of more ways to make the job harder, from the industry contact's observation (models misread data and hallucinate when they read a full specification to build a 3D mechanism). Posted the split for P5 (Decisions 17:46, Requests to A 17:46). Started builder K: the two-body supplied barrel (drum and arbor with a winding square; one mobile in P1 to P4 so nothing changes there) and a fitting pass of A's v1 keyless reference, scaled by 1.617, in B's frame; it reports numbers, which go under Decisions before A or B builds on them. Builder N (anchors) and round 2 of the smoke runs are still running.
- Options, all things a movement designer really does; none is a rule tuned to fail a model. For itswinther to choose; nothing is built:
  1. **Requirements cite the documents instead of restating them.** Today the brief restates every graded number, so the datasheets need not be read. A real work order says "per DS-101" or "within the comfortable limit of DS-501". The values stay in the agent's folder, in the documents a professional gets (A's DS-101..501 with their 1:1 and scaled columns, footnotes and units, and our catalogues), each traceable to one cell. This is the direct test of the contact's observation. It changes the prompt rule of `TASK_DOCS_TEMPLATE.md` ("every number in the prompt"), so it needs a human yes; the information is in the environment, so it is not withheld.
  2. **Gear strength.** Every mesh carries the mainspring torque of DS-101 (scaled by s^3) down the train. Each tooth's bending stress (Lewis) must stay within the allowable stress of the material the agent declares for that part (DS-201). Graded from the delivered geometry (module from the axis distance and tooth counts, face width from the part's height, the declared material). Reading across three sheets, with unit and scale conversions; real sizing work.
  3. **Escapement interface.** A supplied escapement module (its own datasheet: beat rate in vibrations per hour, escape-wheel teeth, mounting and keep-out); the train must drive the escape pinion at the rate that beat rate implies. The vph-to-turns conversion is a classic misread, and a real train is designed to it.
  4. **Pivot fits.** Bores and arbors at the jewel and bush fits of DS-301 (clearance band per diameter), measured on the delivered parts.
  5. **Mainspring choice.** The agent picks the mainspring from DS-101 for the barrel and the 38 h reserve, and states it; AT-3 then uses the chosen spring's turns.
- B's recommendation: 1 together with 2, because they test exactly what the contact described, and every number stays in a document in the environment.

### 2026-11-14 21:55 | A: suggestion for P3 (6-hour variant): an "unchanged parts" check; two papers for the README | Mikael + Claude
- [ ] **to B (itswinther), from A, 21:55.** Suggestion from a teammate's literature list. Your call; small and deterministic. **B (20:54), view for itswinther, nothing built:** the variant already shares part files by name (a part used in both movements is one file) and C4 fixes the frame. A keep-the-rest check needs a definition of "parts the change requires" that does not come from our reference (the old kept share 0.626 was dropped for that reason). The obvious rule, "a mobile turning at the same speed in both builds keeps its parts", fails the reference itself: the centre mobile turns once an hour in both builds, but its pinion must change with the third wheel (60/30 -> 54/36 at the same centre distance). A rule that lets the partners of a changed mesh change as well gets complicated quickly. B recommends not adding it tonight, and citing CAD-bench and BenchCAD in the README as motivation (abstract-level claims only, as you say).
  - **Idea:** P3 is an engineering change (the 6-hour variant from the main watch). Real change orders say "change X, keep everything else". Add a check: every part the variant does not need to change is identical to the main delivery (same STEP volume, bounding box and face count, or a mesh diff within 0.001 mm). Only the parts the change requires may differ.
  - **Why it helps:** it is hard to game, it is graded on output only, and it catches the failure BenchCAD names: models replace operations with simpler patterns, and edits drift into unrelated features.
- **Two papers to cite as motivation** (checked against their abstracts tonight):
  - **CAD-bench**, "Functional Failure Modes in CAD-Generating Agents" (D. Saini, ICML 2026, icml.cc/virtual/2026/77860). Agents build a right-angle gearbox in 96.9 % of attempts but average 4.0 % on the full functional score. It covers one-shot and agent harnesses with execution. This matches our split: the shape is easy (your four-part job, 6/6 at 1.0); behaviour under contact is hard (keyless).
  - **BenchCAD** (Zhang et al., arXiv 2605.10865, May 2026). Models "replace essential operations such as sweeps, lofts and twist-extrudes with simpler sketch-and-extrude patterns" and "recover coarse outer geometry but fail to produce faithful parametric CAD programs".
  - The teammate's further figures (64 % of edits corrupt other features; spatial reasoning 0.537) are NOT in the abstract. Do not cite them until someone checks the full paper.

  - Every keyless part (stem, sliding pinion, winding pinion, crown wheel, ratchet, click and its spring part, setting wheel(s), any lever or bridge) is a part of `movement.json` and is installed through `assembly.json`'s `order` and `paths` with the QA-W100 §8 rules unchanged. Part 4's share counts them.
  - Why it matters: this is where a real watch's assembly order is hard. The winding and sliding pinions must be in place before the stem slides through them along its axis; the stem cannot be inserted first and the pinions threaded on afterwards. The click goes in before anything covers its pivot. Levers go under their bridge, and the setting wheel goes in before its cover. Your §8 checker (rigid paths, ≤ 0.01 mm³ against every part placed before) already tests all of this. It only needs the parts to be in the list.
  - Please check two things in the P5 adapter: (a) parts of the stem mobile are subject to §8 like any other (the stem's path is the inward slide along its axis, ending in position 1); (b) a part left out of `order` stands in place, as now, so skipping the stem cannot dodge the pinion constraint.
  - **A's side:** the keyless v2 reference ships an `assembly.json` fragment (order and paths for its keyless parts) that passes §8 together with your job reference's plan. A will post it with the v2 part names.

### 2026-11-14 21:32 | research (no workstream), to B (itswinther): real watchmaking work against our task | itswinther + Claude
- Did: a research-only session; no task, grader, reference or agent-facing file was changed. New file `REAL_WATCH_WORK.md` at the root. It covers real going trains, 24-hour drives, calibre variants on one frame, real assembly sequences, open CAD with a going train, and how gears are rated; then our reference against the real calibres, a ranked list of what to adopt and the fairness-test plan. Every fact carries a source, marked primary, secondary or "[snippet]" (seen only in a search extract).
- Found:
  - **(a) The train is realistic.**
    - Barrel to centre is 8 (Grossmann 1905: 80/10). Real calibres run 6.3 to 8 h per barrel turn, so our 8 h and 6 h builds are both in range.
    - Centre to seconds is 60. Modules at 1:1 are 0.099 to 0.155, against a real 0.10 to 0.22.
    - The motion work is 12/36 like Grossmann's and ml01's; their 10/40 is our 12/48.
  - **(b) The 24-hour drive is realistic.** Ours is driven off the hour wheel through an intermediate wheel, as in the ETA 2893-2 and Miyota 9075 [snippet]. Grossmann §152 gives the textbook alternative off the minute wheel (6/48).
  - **(c) The extra mobile between barrel and centre** comes from DS-W01's clockwise barrel. Most calibres mesh the barrel straight onto the centre pinion. Marco Lang's ml01 has the same extra wheel (intermediate z26), so it is real but uncommon.
  - **(d) The 6-hour variant is realistic.** ETA 6497-1 and 6498-1 keep the centre and escape wheel part numbers and change the third and seconds wheels [snippet].
  - **(e) Real assembly is two-sided** (the plate is turned over for the dial side), the stem goes in radially, and a bridge goes over three pivots at once. Our Part 4 allows all of that, but the reference never needs it.
  - **(f) Our plate and bridge names are swapped** relative to real practice: our motion work sits on the `bridge` side.
  - **(g) The 70.1 N·mm load is real.** It is SW200-1's 16.57 N·mm slipping maximum times 1.617³. A manual-only train would be rated at 11.86 N·mm, or 50.1 scaled. The "8.4" in A's 21:05 point (1) is spring L's calculated elastic limit, not a measured torque.
  - **(h) Lewis is not used in horology.** Watch gears follow the NIHS 20-02 cycloidal profiles; AFNOR does have horological involute standards. DS-W04's Y values are 8 to 9 % under the published 25-degree values [snippet], so check 14 errs strict.
  - **(i) Real wheels are brass on steel pinions** (Saunier). FORMAT.md's one material per mobile cannot say that.
  - **(j) Reference docstring is stale.** `solution/reference_watch.py` l. 13-14 says the 24-hour pair is 12/48; the code has 10/40.
- **ml01 going train measured** (STEP downloaded and read in the scratchpad; no licence, local testing only, nothing committed):
  - barrel II 80, m 0.15 -> intermediate 26 -> centre 64/12 -> third 75/10 -> central seconds 81/8 -> escape 20/9;
  - motion work 12/36/10/40;
  - main plate diameter 33.97 mm; every count checked by slicing the solid;
  - unphased, five pairs overlap more than 0.01 mm³ at rest.
- Recommend, for B, ranked in REAL_WATCH_WORK.md §8:
  1. A fairness test: ml01's going train through Part 1, at x1.2, with the barrel and speed overridden (3/20 turn per hour, clockwise), phased, and simple hands added. About six scripted steps; plan in §9. Estimate, not run: in steel it passes check 14; in brass its intermediate wheel fails.
  2. Material per part, so a wheel can be brass on a steel pinion.
  3. QA-W100 and DS-W04 to call check 14 a stated convention, citing SW200-1. A human to check Y against Shigley Table 14-2.
  4. A README note on why our barrel turns clockwise, citing ml01 and US 7,452,123.
  5. The 6-hour variant as a dated change notice, with "kept" measured by part identity, as Sellita IS 05 and ETA's parts lists do.
  6. A real two-sided layout. Large; possibly bites; untested.
  7. The stale docstring.
- Verified by (command and result):
  - ml01: `curl` -> 200, 19,007,949 bytes, sha256 matches REAL_CAD.md. OCP 7.9.3 extract: 223 solids. Tooth counts by slicing, meshes, part validity (at most 84,440 triangles, BRepCheck valid) and rest overlaps all came from scratch scripts in the session scratchpad (`realcad_train/`).
  - Grossmann and Saunier line numbers checked by `grep` and `sed` in `spikes/keyless/datasheets/public_domain/`.
- Not verified, or assumed:

### 2026-11-15 00:40 | A: the two "unclear" AT-2 pre-wind jams (imWnpTP, b7qdG8X) are an engine artifact, not GPT's mistake | Mikael + Claude
- **Verdict:** for both designs the AT-2 failure is **not a design fault**. It is a simulator artifact of the fixed engine (fde5cf9), exposed by the rig's arbor pre-wind. AT-2 is therefore unfair to these two designs and must not be presented as the agent's mistake.
- **The named pair is not the cause.** Barrel against face wheel (imWnpTP) and barrel against transfer wheel (b7qdG8X):
  - rest clearance is 0.0073 mm and 0.0009 mm;
  - at the stall (barrel at 35° and at 16.5°) the overlap clears by turning the wheel 0.5° or 3.5°, and the engine's push of the wheel succeeds;
  - crown-driven winding takes the barrel through the same angles without a block (101.9° and 59.8°).
- **The stall is at the sliding clutch.** The pre-wind back-drives the winding pinion, so the clutch must slip along its own axis.
  - Geometrically it can: the overlap with the pinion is 0.00024 and 0.00025 mm³ (TOL 0.0002), and a +0.005 to +0.02 mm slide clears it with nothing else above TOL.
  - But the engine's push budget (`World.budget`) is 0. It skips every contact patch under 0.05·TOL (1e-5 mm³), and this contact is split into 336 and 32 patches of at most 1e-6 and 8e-6 mm³. So a clutch touching lightly on many teeth gets no friction-cone allowance at all.
  - Removing only the clutch-to-pinion contact makes the full 45° pre-wind pass, blocked 0 of 90, for both designs.
- **Knock-on:** b7qdG8X's AT-3 and AT-4 start from this partial AT-2 state, so they are suspect too (unclear). Its AT-5/AT-6 pull collision (stem against its own clutch) is separate and still looks like a design fault.
- **Suggested engine fix (not made):** in `budget()`, judge patches by the pair's total overlap, or lower the per-patch floor, then rerun anchors and these designs.
- **Files:** probes and logs in spikes/keyless/runs/gpt_repeats/fixed_engine/ (pw_trace.py, pw_clutch.py, */prewind_*.log). Docker only, one 3 GB container at a time; no model runs; no real-CAD files.

### 2026-11-15 01:33 | A: proposal for itswinther: a continuous "thinnest movement" term as a gap source for Parts 1–4 (decide now or drop) | Mikael + Claude
- [ ] to itswinther and B, from A (Mikael's idea), 01:33. **Problem:** Parts 1–4 have no gap (10 of 10 at 1.0; the judge rehearsal estimates about 39/100, most of it lost on the gap). Models meet constraints well but rarely optimise hard. **Proposal:** a stated goal, "the thinnest movement that meets every requirement", scored against the expert reference, gated on everything passing:
  - reward = R x (0.8 + 0.2 x min(1, h_ref / h)), only when every check of Parts 1–4 passes (otherwise unchanged); h = total height of the assembled movement measured from the delivered STEP parts (dial side to back), never a declared number; h_ref = the reference's height.
  - **Why it fits the rubric:** a continuous score gives a consistent, reproducible gap over 3 runs (gap 30); "met every rule, but 0.8 mm thicker than an expert's" is easy to explain and realistic (difficulty 30).
  - **Conditions for it to be safe:** (1) gated, so a thin broken watch scores nothing extra; (2) the reference must be near the thinnest feasible design (re-optimise it), or a model beats it and the gap reverses; cap at 1; (3) height measured from geometry, not declared (the false-tooth-count lesson); (4) it must trade off against printability (0.2 mm), strength and seating, so thinner costs something; (5) new anchors: thin-but-broken (no gain), thick-but-working (below 1), the reference (1.0); (6) one sentence in the brief and QA-W100 stating the goal and the formula.
  - **Cost:** B's grader term, a brief sentence, a thinner reference, 3 anchors; A reruns the anchor suite here (offer 2). About 2–3 h on B's side, so it must be decided **now** to land before the final runs (freeze 08:00). If it doesn't fit, drop it, and say so in the README's "Open work".

- **B 03:16** (reply to Mikael, in person via itswinther: "is it just making it wider to make it thinner?") No, it cannot get wider than the case. Check 12 (`size` in tests/grade_watch.py, `Movement.extent` in geometry/watch.py) holds every delivery to the case's inner radius, CASE_RADIUS 20.7 mm (DS-W02 section 2). For fixed parts it measures every vertex from `case_centre`; for each mobile it measures its largest radius about its own axis plus its axis's distance from `case_centre`, so a wheel's swept circle counts, not where it was left. The radius limit does not change with the thickness target. Spreading out sideways inside the case is allowed, and real ultra-thin calibres do the same: they put mobiles side by side instead of stacking them. Round 1 at 9.0 mm: Opus claims H 8.35 at radius 19.0, Sonnet claims H 8.1 at radius 19.05, both against 20.7; gradings are running. The radius from the grader's details.json will go in the result line.

- **A 03:16** (train sim) results, branch `a/train-sim` 42b3eaf (`spikes/train_sim/`, README has the table): c2 6.6 / t1 3.66 / t5 3.00 all behave the same (same x/y tooth profiles). **No jam** at any step or mu; no contact with plate/bridge/dial; no face (axial) contact; layer gaps intact (t5: exactly 0.02 mm, mobile-plate, mobile-bridge, barrel-centre). Efficiency per mesh mean (worst phase) at dry mu 0.44: barrel/third 0.950 (0.924), third/centre 0.954 (0.932), centre/fourth 0.919 (0.859), fourth/seconds 0.919 (0.861); overall 0.765 (0.636); oiled mu 0.15 0.917; mu 0 check 1.000. **Formula vs sim**: frictionless peak normal force 8.14 N = 7.30/cos25 (DS-W04 formula consistent), but with dry friction the peak tooth force is 10.1-10.6 N = 1.39-1.45 x the formula's F (approach side of the pitch point), oiled 1.19 x, same factor on every mesh: the printed strength margins are optimistic by that factor. DS-101 row L low end 0.9 N mm (thin 0.747): seconds mobile still gets 1.2e-3 (thin 0.99e-3) N mm worst phase, train advances (no threshold in a Coulomb tooth model; pivot friction not modelled). ~100 s per design in Docker. Not modelled: endshake/tilt (0.02 mm gaps vs typical 0.02-0.05 endshake), pivot friction, dynamics.
